user
Integrated wiki, with run+time use, design-time features, and debug mode functionality
assistant
An **integrated wiki** system—used at runtime, design-time, and in debug—can be a *powerful connective tissue* between your game’s emotional, narrative, and appearance systems. It can support both authors and players by contextualizing choices, tracking facts, and surfacing lore and relationships in meaningful ways.

Below is a structured breakdown of how such a system could work across its three intended modes.

---

## 🧠 Overview: What the Integrated Wiki Is

| Aspect              | Description |
|---------------------|-------------|
| **Purpose**         | A living, context-aware knowledge system embedded in the game engine and tools |
| **Access Modes**    | Design-time (authoring), Runtime (player-facing), Debug (dev testing & introspection) |
| **Key Content Types** | Characters, Locations, Events, Emotions, Appearance Tags, Cultures, Rules, Lore, etc. |
| **Update Sources**  | Author input, in-game triggers, event results, mod metadata |

---

## 🎮 Runtime Use (Player-Facing)

This mode supports **players** by surfacing contextual, emotional, and narrative data **dynamically as they progress**.

### 🧾 Core Features:
- **Auto-updating entries** as events unfold (e.g., “You met Arel at the ball. She seemed tired.”)
- **Emotion-aware descriptions** (e.g., trust: 22, jealousy: 40)
- **Appearance-based memory** (e.g., “He wore mourning black when he delivered the news.”)
- **Timeline of notable interactions**
- **Relationship maps**, optionally gated by in-world knowledge (i.e., *the player* doesn’t know someone’s grudge exists yet)
- **Event summaries** (e.g., “You sabotaged 3 uniforms. They were missing insignia.”)

### 🧩 Optional UX Features:
- Hover info (tooltip overlays)
- “What they know” vs. “What you know” toggles
- Cultural info snippets relevant to context (“Wearing red means dishonor in their clan.”)

---

## 🛠️ Design-Time Use (Author-Facing)

This mode supports **authors** in creating, organizing, and referencing knowledge *within* the authoring tools.

### 📚 Uses:
- Write and edit canonical character bios, cultural norms, relationship networks, event summaries, etc.
- Link emotional or appearance tags directly to entries
- **Contextual linking** from events to related data (e.g., highlight a character’s name in a script and jump to their wiki entry)
- Use wiki tags and filters as **selector sources** in event conditions
- Embed emotional or decay profile metadata in the entry (e.g., "This character is reactive and prone to jealousy")

### 🔧 Editor Tool Features:
- Markdown or rich text wiki editor
- Live preview
- Schema enforcement or templates (e.g., all character pages include: appearance, emotion_profile, cultural_background, etc.)
- Cross-referenced appearance rules or selector logic snippets
- Searchable with alias and tag support

---

## 🧪 Debug Mode (Dev-Facing)

In this mode, the wiki becomes an **introspective, diagnostic, and analytics tool** during QA, automated testing, or mod development.

### 🔍 Debug Wiki Features:
- **Live update display** of character emotional states, memory snapshots, appearance logs
- Show **resolved selector queries** (e.g., “why didn’t this event fire?” → actor X lacked outfit tag Y)
- View **mod-provided wiki entries** with conflict resolution markers
- Show **entry diff logs** (who/what updated this page and when)
- Export current state as a report (e.g., for bug tracking)
- Filter by visibility: what the player sees vs. what is hidden

---

## 📦 Data Model Considerations

Wiki entries should support:
```json
{
  "id": "character_arel",
  "type": "character",
  "display_name": "Arel Varn",
  "content": "Arel is a junior ambassador from the eastern court. She has a history with fire magic.",
  "tags": ["magic_user", "formal", "jealous"],
  "linked_entries": ["culture_eastern", "event_embassy_gala"],
  "dynamic_fields": {
    "last_seen": "container_event_royal_ball",
    "current_mood": "anxious",
    "appearance": {
      "outfit": "formal",
      "accessories": ["opal_pendant"]
    }
  },
  "visibility": {
    "player_knows_name": true,
    "player_knows_history": false
  }
}
```

---

## 🔌 Mod Support Integration

Mods should be able to:
- **Add wiki entries** (e.g., new factions, cultural practices, invented characters)
- **Link to or override** existing entries
- **Supply localization hooks** for entries
- Mark **which systems or selectors** they extend

You might want to show mod status per entry in debug view or allow mod-specific “namespaces” (e.g., `modname:entry_id`).

---

| Value | Description |
|-------|-------------|
| 🧠 Knowledge Clarity | Helps players and authors track complex interwoven state |
| 🔄 World Consistency | Reinforces cultural, emotional, and relational logic |
| 🧪 Testing Insight | Surfaces the “why” behind engine behavior |
| 🛠 Authoring Speed | Centralizes reference material during content design |
| 🎮 Player Immersion | Keeps emotional and story continuity visible and coherent |

---

## ✅ Summary: Integrated Wiki by Mode

| Mode       | Key Use                         | Highlight Feature                      |
|------------|----------------------------------|----------------------------------------|
| **Runtime** | Support player comprehension     | Emotional snapshots, relationship maps |
| **Design**  | Help authors maintain cohesion   | Taggable entries with schema templates |
| **Debug**   | Provide introspection & testing  | Live mood logs, selector diagnostics   |

user
What are the trade-offs between putting work into making the wiki skinnable versus making it primarily an API for authors to use in their custom UI?
assistant
This is a smart question, and one that reflects a mature approach to tooling design. The choice between **investing in a skinnable, built-in wiki UI** versus **offering a flexible wiki API for author-driven presentation** is fundamentally about **control vs. convenience**, **effort vs. flexibility**, and **audience vs. ecosystem**.

Below is a breakdown of the key trade-offs across multiple dimensions:

---

## 🧩 Comparison Table

| Factor | Skinnable Built-In UI | API-First for Author Custom UI |
|--------|------------------------|----------------------------------|
| **Ease of Use** | ✅ High – turnkey solution | ⚠️ Depends on author skill/UI stack |
| **Customization** | ⚠️ Limited to theme/skin scope | ✅ Full control over layout/behavior |
| **Development Effort (You)** | ⚠️ High – you must design, style, maintain UI | ✅ Lower – only expose structured data & hooks |
| **Support for Mixed-Culture Casts / Custom Rules** | ⚠️ Needs lots of UI flexibility work | ✅ Easily integrated into story-specific UI |
| **Theming & Brand Integration** | ⚠️ Constrained to predefined themes | ✅ Unlimited (custom colors, fonts, animations) |
| **Player-Facing Consistency** | ✅ Uniform across all games using the engine | ⚠️ Varies wildly unless standardized by project |
| **Tooling & Debug Views** | ✅ Easier to include with internal debug UI | ⚠️ Requires separate debug UI or overlays |
| **Modder Support** | ✅ Simple if you support dynamic entry registration | ⚠️ More complexity unless modders adopt project’s UI |
| **Accessibility (A11y)** | ⚠️ On you to build in features like screen reader support | ⚠️ On the author—but some engines handle this |
| **Localization Handling** | ✅ Can provide standard i18n hooks | ⚠️ Must be integrated manually by the author |
| **Performance Control** | ⚠️ Harder to optimize across arbitrary projects | ✅ Up to the game UI—can be tuned per need |

---

## 🧠 Strategic Pros & Cons

### 🎨 Skinnable UI – Pros
- Authors get a working, styled interface **out of the box**
- Great for **prototypes**, internal tools, and smaller teams
- **Player UX is consistent** across games using your system
- Easier to **embed debug, devtools, or documentation** in a unified way

### 🎨 Skinnable UI – Cons
- Adds **surface area for bugs**, testing, localization, and theming effort
- May be too **opinionated or inflexible** for stylized or branded games
- Harder to meet every author's narrative tone or visual direction
- Increased maintenance if future visual or interaction standards shift

---

### 🧱 API-First – Pros
- **Maximum flexibility** for authors to align with their game's tone, style, and UX
- Simplifies your job: just provide a well-documented, observable data layer
- Works well with **custom emotional systems**, appearance rules, mod behaviors
- Encourages innovation—some authors will do incredible things with it

### 🧱 API-First – Cons
- Some authors will be overwhelmed by lack of prebuilt options
- Harder to ensure **consistency or best practices** across games
- May result in poor UX if not implemented well by individual devs
- Less immediate value without **example UIs or plugin helpers**

---

## 🚦Recommendation: **Hybrid Layered Approach**

You don’t have to pick just one. Here’s a high-leverage strategy:

### ✅ 1. Build a **Clean, Extensible Wiki API Layer**
- Pure data: entries, tags, relationship links, visibility, localization
- Full event-driven structure (e.g., `on_wiki_entry_updated`, `on_visibility_changed`)
- Include export/import features for modding and save state inspection

### ✅ 2. Provide a **Reference UI** (Optional, Skinnable)
- Basic but complete UI shipped with the engine/plugin
- Meant for prototyping, inspiration, debugging, or smaller games
- Styled using standard skin/theme options (e.g., Godot’s Theme resource)
- Modular: allow authors to disable certain panels (e.g., emotions tab)

### ✅ 3. Offer **Hooks and Visual Aids** for Custom UIs
- Reactive components (`WikiEntryPanel`, `WikiLink`, `WikiTooltip`)
- Visual debug overlays (e.g., highlight characters linked to active wiki topics)
- Template-driven entry rendering (`Markdown + tag interpreters`)

This hybrid model supports:
- 🧪 **Debug & testing tooling**  
- 🎮 **Small projects with no design team**  
- 🎨 **Custom UIs for polished or stylized games**  
- 🧱 **Modular plugin and mod compatibility**

---

## Would You Like Help With:
- Designing a structured data schema for wiki entries and linkages?
- Planning event-driven communication between the wiki and other subsystems?
- Drafting a minimal, themeable reference UI for wiki browsing?

The **API-first, skin-optional** model gives you the best of both worlds and fits beautifully with the modular, author-friendly philosophy already present in your project.

For a Godot plugin, the internal base name should follow conventions that are:

- ✅ **Lowercase and underscore-based** (for directory and script names)  
- ✅ **Scoped and descriptive**  
- ✅ **Unique enough** to avoid collision in the wider plugin ecosystem  
- ✅ **Consistent across directory structure, script classes, and Godot settings**

### 📦 `chordline`  
Simple, clean, directly mirrors the project name. Use this as your:
- `addons/chordline/` directory  
- Root namespace for autoloads or modules  
- Base prefix for settings: `chordline/enable_debug_ui`, `chordline/wiki/theme`

## 📦 Plugin Registration Strategy

- Each subsystem has its own `plugin.cfg` and `*.gd` root
- The core handles:
  - Schema definitions (characters, emotions, appearance tags, relationships)
  - System lifecycle coordination (load order, cross-system messaging)
  - Global interfaces (signals like `on_character_updated`, `on_tick_advanced`)
- Subsystems **register themselves to the core** at `_ready()`, similar to how Godot handles autoloads or custom nodes

---

## 🔌 Optional Subsystems as Plugins

You can make the following systems **optional** (opt-in via plugin UI or dependency config):

| Subsystem        | Description |
|------------------|-------------|
| `appearance/`    | Outfits, visibility rules, selector hooks |
| `wiki/`          | Runtime + design-time dynamic lore/wiki tracking |
| `debug/`         | Overlays, emotion/mood logging, event replay |
| `containers/`    | Event nesting, duration-scoped logic, layered triggers |
| `tools/`         | Editor-facing authoring panels or scene inspectors |

---

## 🔧 Godot Integration Considerations

### Plugin Activation
- `core/plugin.cfg` can activate the system itself
- Subsystems are auto-detected or enabled via `project.godot` settings (`chordline/enable_emotion`)

### Signals & Registration
- Core exposes:
  ```gdscript
  signal chordline_ready
  signal subsystem_registered(name)
  ```
- Subsystems emit:
  ```gdscript
  emit_signal("subsystem_registered", "appearance")
  ```

### Naming Consistency
- Classes: `ChordlineEmotionManager`, `ChordlineWikiEntry`, etc.
- Signals: `chordline/emotion/updated`, `chordline/appearance/changed`
- Settings: `chordline/wiki/enable_tooltips`, `chordline/relationships/default_decay`

---

## 📑 Documentation Benefits

This modular structure also allows you to:
- Document each subsystem as a standalone component
- Include versioning (e.g., `relationships v1.2`)
- Let developers or modders fork just the parts they want to extend
- Publish subsystems separately in Godot’s Asset Library (e.g., *Chordline Core*, *Chordline: Emotion Engine*)

Yes, you're understanding it correctly—**Chordline Core** should serve as the **foundational layer** of the system, focused on shared structures and coordination. Here's a more explicit breakdown of what the **core** is and *is not*, relative to the optional plugin subsystems:

---

## ✅ **Chordline Core** — What It *Is*

The **Core** is the **minimal necessary engine** to support:

### 1. 🧠 **Foundational Data Models**
- Characters
- Relationships (basic form)
- Emotions and moods (interface, not logic)
- Appearance state container
- Metadata for scenes, ticks, and state transitions
- Localization context and character cultural profile tags

### 2. 🔄 **Event System Backbone**
- Event representation (structure, scope, priority, timing)
- Ticking system with variable-duration tick control
- Actor selectors (property-based, appearance-based, emotional, etc.)
- Event result application (with plug-in hooks)
- Actor pool & context resolution

### 3. 🛠️ **Design Studio Hooks**
- Data schema definitions (used by editors)
- Signals to drive custom UI extensions (`character_changed`, `event_fired`, etc.)
- A generic debug console & logging panel (basic)
- Integration points for tools (e.g. mod explorer, wiki author)

### 4. 📡 **Subsystem Interface Contracts**
- Registry or service pattern for things like:
  - `register_emotion_engine(engine_ref)`
  - `get_selector_results(type, parameters)`
- Communication between systems via signals or bus-like routing

---

## 🚫 **Chordline Core** — What It *Is Not*

The core should **not** directly implement:

| Feature | Why it belongs elsewhere |
|--------|---------------------------|
| **Emotion decay rules** | Handled by `emotion/` plugin—could be replaced or customized |
| **Appearance selectors** | Reside in `appearance/`, may differ by game or culture |
| **Wiki or Lore system** | `wiki/` is opt-in—some games may not use it |
| **Container event nesting** | `containers/` plugin handles scoping, blocking, and parallelism |
| **Custom localization UIs** | May vary per game; `tools/` or `l10n/` plugin extends base |
| **Character archetypes** | Best stored in a mod-friendly data registry, separate from core |
| **Debug overlay UI** | `debug/` provides toggleable inspector/overlay widgets |

---

## 🔌 Plugins Connect *To* the Core

Each subsystem plugin registers itself to the core at runtime, e.g.:

```gdscript
Chordline.get_instance().register_subsystem("emotion", self)
```

This pattern allows:
- Subsystems to query the core (e.g., `get_character_data(id)`)
- Core events to be observed or modified by subsystems (e.g., “before mood is applied”)
- Modders to insert new logic without touching internal engine code

---

## 🔁 Example: Runtime Flow

When an event is triggered:
1. Core resolves selector logic (via plugin-provided selectors)
2. Core applies results (e.g. mood changes, appearance shifts)
3. Core emits signals
4. `emotion/`, `appearance/`, and `wiki/` respond accordingly
5. `debug/` logs all actions if enabled

---

## 🔧 Summary

| Layer      | Responsibility                                 |
|------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| `core/`    | Models, event execution, ticks, integration hooks |
| `emotion/` | Decay logic, short- vs long-term affect         |
| `appearance/` | Outfit tags, visual selectors, state reactions |
| `wiki/`    | In-world knowledge base, design/runtime/docs     |
| `containers/` | Event nesting, scoping, concurrency            |
| `debug/`   | Logs, introspection, test surfaces               |
| `tools/`   | Authoring UIs, mod editors, custom inspectors    |

---

You're sketching out a **well-structured, privacy-conscious, and decentralized model** for optional network-based features—especially thoughtful for open-source and community-facing development. Below is a breakdown of your ideas and their design implications, organized with purpose, rationale, and implementation recommendations.

---

## 🌐 Overview: Optional Network-Based Features

| Feature                     | Purpose                                  | Notes |
|-----------------------------|------------------------------------------|-------|
| **Game data file updates**  | Distribute content patches, mod data     | Engine stays manually updated |
| **Anonymized metrics**      | Feedback on player behavior, emotional paths, bug surfaces | Opt-in per-game + per-save |
| **Cloud saves**             | Cross-device or recovery-based continuity | Controlled by project authority |
| **Strict authority model**  | Prevents centralizing under engine vendor | Authority defined per game |
| **Server components**       | Modular, open, and versioned             | Built from the main project |

---

## 🧠 Design Goals

- **Modularity** – every network feature is opt-in at all levels
- **Transparency** – player and author can inspect and override behavior
- **Privacy** – zero tracking unless explicitly allowed
- **Federation-friendly** – game devs can host their own authority
- **Author-centric** – supports custom behaviors per narrative/game

---

## 🧩 Detailed Breakdown

### 📦 1. **Game Data File Update**
**Purpose**: Allow automatic updates for narrative content, character definitions, emotion decay profiles, etc.

**Key Principles:**
- 🔒 Engine updates are always manual (to prevent security risks)
- ✅ Game/mod content may be pulled automatically (if enabled)
- ⚙️ Uses signed or hashed packages with version checks
- 🧩 Source is defined by game’s authority, not the engine

**Implementation Ideas:**
```yaml
updates:
  enabled: true
  source_url: "https://chordline.example.com/data/"
  last_checked: "2025-07-02"
```

- Local storage caches source metadata
- Optional diff tools or validation hashes for checking compatibility

---

### 📊 2. **Anonymized Metrics System**

**Purpose**: Understand how emotional arcs, choices, and scenes are navigated by players—without invading privacy.

**Key Features:**
- 🔄 Opt-in at:
  - 🌍 Global (engine setting)
  - 💾 Per-save (data flag)
- 🔐 Local GUID generated per save (not device-level)
- 📊 Local UI shows what’s being collected
- 👀 Can view *global aggregates* without uploading data

**Data Types:**
```json
{
  "guid": "anon-9f5a12",
  "events_triggered": ["ballroom_intro", "duel"],
  "emotions_max": {"jealousy": 60, "trust": 15},
  "paths_chosen": ["route_A", "route_C"],
  "duration_ticks": 312
}
```

**Server Considerations:**
- Minimal: flat-file or JSON aggregate store
- Daily/weekly snapshot publishing
- Self-hostable per game or mod

---

### ☁️ 3. **Cloud Saves**

**Purpose**: Player convenience, multi-device play, or soft fallback for narrative-heavy games.

**Key Features:**
- 🔐 Tied to save slot + optional local encryption
- 🌐 Upload requires active opt-in per-save
- 🗄 Server-controlled—not managed by engine or shared network
- 🛠 Could use Git-style hashes to sync + diff saves
- 💼 Compatible with team testing (dev cloud saves per branch)

---

### 🛂 4. **Authority Control**

**Design Philosophy**:  
All networked features defer to a **game-defined authority**—**not** a central engine authority or plugin-level service.

**Implications:**
- Each game or mod project can:
  - Define endpoints for updates, metrics, saves
  - Disable networking entirely
- Encourages **federated or decentralized game ecosystems**
- Avoids GDPR/CCPA nightmares for plugin maintainers

---

### 🧱 5. **Standalone Server Components**

**Required Capabilities:**
- Built from same codebase or data model
- Version-controlled API schema per feature (e.g. `metrics/v1`, `cloud/v2`)
- Can run locally for testing/dev workflows
- Ideally language-agnostic wrappers (e.g., RESTful spec + optional Godot server or Python/Node service)

**Suggested Server Modules:**
- `metrics_collector/`
- `update_registry/`
- `cloud_save_store/`

**Nice-to-haves:**
- CLI: `chordline-server --dev --serve-cloud --serve-metrics`
- Configurable data expiry / aggregation intervals
- Hosted on flat file systems, S3, or lightweight DBs

---

## ✅ Summary of Network Features and Controls

| Feature         | Controlled By     | Opt-In Levels      | Notes |
|----------------|-------------------|--------------------|-------|
| Game updates    | Project authority | Global             | Core patching always manual |
| Metrics         | Project authority | Global + Per-Save  | Anonymous, local preview, view-only mode |
| Cloud saves     | Project authority | Per-Save           | Optional encryption, version tracking |
| Networking      | Not plugin-wide   | Feature-by-feature | Total disable possible |

---

## 🌍 Future-Proofing Considerations

- Federated or local-only “authority modes”
- Signed metrics bundle uploads (prevent tampering)
- Modular server plugin architecture (each server-side feature is optional)
- Support for custom UIs on the server (e.g., visualize global mood arc distribution)

## 🔄 Cross-Game Save Loading

### 🧠 Core Idea  
Allow a save file or featured community save from **Game A** to be used as **input** to **Game B**, even if the second game has a different world, scope, or engine logic—as long as the relevant emotional or relational schema is **compatible**.

---

## 📦 What Transfers Between Games?

### 🧱 Structural Layers

| Layer                    | Transferable? | Notes |
|--------------------------|---------------|-------|
| **Character IDs**        | ✅ Optional mapping allowed |
| **Relationship baselines** | ✅ May need translation layer |
| **Emotional baselines**   | ✅ Custom schema translation |
| **Story flags / branches** | ⚠️ Only if explicitly supported |
| **Appearance tags**      | ✅ With asset translation |
| **Culture/profile info** | ✅ Good candidate for narrative carryover |
| **Per-save metadata**    | ✅ Version, title, GUID, etc. |
| **Moods**                | ❌ Optional, usually reset in a new game |

---

## 🛠 How It Works

### 🧰 Author Flow
1. **Define a “Save Import Schema”** in Game B
2. Create a **character mapping file** (e.g., `"protagonist_id_A" → "protagonist_id_B"`)
3. Set up **translation rules** for emotions, relationships, cultural tags, etc.
4. Decide what events or modifiers are **triggered by import context**

### 🎮 Player Flow
1. On new game start, user selects a prior save or featured community save
2. The game:
   - Translates imported data via declared schema
   - Triggers specific story states, opening variations, or locked/unlocked content
3. The player continues from a world that "remembers"

---

## 💡 Example Scenarios

- **Sequel**: Game B recognizes that the protagonist left Game A trusting Character X. This unlocks a different introduction scene.
- **Tightly episodic release**: Game A ends in a cliffhanger; Game B loads the actual world state and continues seamlessly.
- **Modular anthology**: Several short stories share a "world echo" mechanic—actions in one color the emotional background of the next.
- **Multiverse variant**: Load a save from another universe where your character made different emotional choices. Game reacts accordingly.

---

## 🧰 Required Support Tools

### For Developers:
- Schema diff/translation tool (`save_v1_to_v2_map.json`)
- ID mapping helpers (characters, appearances, tags)
- Test harness for simulating imported data

### For Players:
- UI to pick a save from previous game/module
- Save info preview (title, game version, character status summary)
- Clear indication of what is imported

---

## 🧩 Compatibility Modes

| Mode          | Description |
|---------------|-------------|
| **Strict**    | Game B expects a save from a known previous version/ID |
| **Loose**     | Game B accepts any Chordline-compatible save with minimal overlap |
| **Narrative Seed** | Only select metadata (e.g., emotion profile, world state mood) is used to shape Game B's starting conditions |
| **Reference Only** | Player can view/import but the state does not affect gameplay |

---

## 🌍 Federation-Friendly Design

Each game defines:
- Its **own import schema**
- Optional **fallbacks** for unknown data
- Whether **data is modified** or just read
- Whether **external saves** are allowed at all (can be gated)

---

## 🎮 Multiplayer Support – Design Principles

| Design Goal                | Implication                                       |
|----------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|
| ✅ Player-hosted sessions   | Decentralized, moddable, LAN/dev-friendly         |
| ✅ Central server optional  | Suitable for indie scale or mod communities       |
| ✅ Variable tick model      | Enables multiple timing strategies                |
| ✅ Deterministic simulation | Makes rollback, replays, desync checks viable     |
| ✅ Already modular systems  | Emotional and state sync can be subsystem-aware   |

---

## 🧩 Modes of Multiplayer

### 🕓 1. **Asynchronous (Turn-Based or Narrative Push)**
- Players make decisions independently or sequentially
- Shared story state updates when actions are submitted
- Ideal for narrative-focused games or co-op play
- Can be **email-style**, **hotseat**, or **delayed co-op**

**Example**:  
Players each play a day at a formal court, then all scenes resolve emotionally before the next act begins.

---

### 🪟 2. **Windowed Sync**
- Players must act within a time window (e.g., “each must respond within 5 real minutes or X ticks”)
- Ticks only advance when consensus (or quorum) is met
- Allows negotiation-driven or multiplayer branching events

---

### 🏛 3. **Soft Lockstep**
- Players remain synchronized on tick boundaries
- Each tick is deterministic and verifiable across clients
- Ideal for events where **emotions, moods, or outcomes** are influenced by multiple actors at once

**Requires:**
- Tick checkpointing
- Input commitment windows
- Optional rollback (if desyncs occur)

---

### 🧠 4. **Authoritative Independent Clock**
- Some games may run on a master session clock (real or game-world time)
- Players observe and act within this window, but the simulation does not pause
- Events may happen with or without player input

---

## 🌐 Network Architecture Options

| Feature                     | Notes |
|-----------------------------|-------|
| **Peer-hosted**            | Simple dev testing, modded games, LAN, direct play |
| **Federated host**         | A community mod group runs multiple small hosted games |
| **Central server**         | Optional; used for hosted public saves or shared metagames |
| **Authoritative node**     | Can be any of the above, just needs to own simulation clock |

**Bonus**: Your existing modular subsystems can register their own `net_sync()` or `serialize_delta()` behavior.

---

## 🧪 Testing Considerations

You already mentioned:

- ✅ Engine designed for determinism testing
- ✅ Tick system is structured
- ✅ Save and state serialization is consistent

Add:

- 🔁 **Rewind & replay testing** (for rollback or simulation verification)
- 🧩 **Per-subsystem sync verification** (`emotion`, `appearance`, etc.)
- 🧾 **Network trace logs** for comparing decision paths

| Subsystem     | Sync Mode        | Notes |
|---------------|------------------|-------|
| `emotion`     | Deterministic     | Same inputs = same decay, outcomes |
| `appearance`  | Snapshot or delta | Small changes over time; use tags |
| `event`       | Input-driven      | Must sync trigger + resolution choices |
| `wiki`        | Push updates      | Deterministic once changed |
| `containers`  | Time-gated logic  | Must share tick schedule or key window timing |

user
Multiplayer implies the "main character" is no longer unique (now N of them), implying that in the data model they're now more similar to other characters.
assistant
Yes—and you're absolutely right to catch that. **Multiplayer fundamentally redefines the "main character" concept**, and doing it well means shifting your data model and system assumptions to embrace **multiple protagonists**, each potentially with:

- narrative agency  
- emotional persistence  
- partial control over the world  

This **flattens the character hierarchy**, making *all* characters closer to peers in structure—even if some are designated as more player-facing in a given moment or story path.

---

## 🧠 Implications for the Data Model

### ✅ Before Multiplayer:
```yaml
main_character:
  id: player
  mood: {...}
  relationships: {...}
  flags: {...}

other_characters:
  - id: aiko
  - id: jules
```

### ✅ After Multiplayer:
```yaml
characters:
  - id: player1
    is_player_controlled: true
    perspective_enabled: true
  - id: player2
    is_player_controlled: true
    perspective_enabled: true
  - id: aiko
    is_player_controlled: false
    perspective_enabled: false
```

---

## 🔄 Key Changes to Implement

### 1. 🧩 **Player Character Role is Now a Tag**
- `is_player_controlled: true`
- `perspective_enabled: true`  
  (e.g., can see their internal state, make decisions, etc.)

Now the engine doesn’t treat a single "main character" as structurally unique.

---

### 2. 🧠 **Perspective-Relative Data**
- Emotions, thoughts, inner monologue, etc., must be:
  - Stored per-character
  - Rendered only for active perspective

This enables:
- Switching viewpoint between players
- Masking internal state when viewing another player

---

### 3. 📖 **Event Scoping Adjustments**
- Events previously scoped as:
  ```yaml
  target: main_character
  ```
  Should now use:
  ```yaml
  target_selector:
    filter: is_player_controlled == true
  ```

- Optional support for **player-specific branches**:
  ```yaml
  if actor.id == player1:
    ...
  ```

---

### 4. 💬 **Dialogue, UI, and Choice Path Changes**
- Dialog and choice rendering must:
  - Know which player is active
  - Respect individual emotional/mood overlays
  - Route choices to the correct decision owner
- UI likely needs “per-player context mode” and/or a hotseat indicator

---

### 5. 🤝 **Relationships Between Player Characters**
These become first-class and require:
- Support for mood baselines, trust/jealousy/etc. between players
- Player-triggered actions that affect other player-controlled characters
- Event logic that’s symmetric or responsive to two-player dynamics

---

### 6. 🪞 **Mirror States and Shared Conditions**
Some player experiences will need:
- Linked moods (e.g., shared grief, rivalry tension)
- Coordinated outcomes (e.g., both players in same scene)
- Optional *inter-character resonance* effects (e.g., your fear increases if my calm drops)

---

### 7. 🧪 **Debug, QA, and Authoring Tools**
Design studio and test systems need to:
- Represent multiple protagonist states side by side
- Log changes per-player for event resolution
- Simulate branching or timing decisions across multiple perspectives

---

## ✅ Summary: System-Level Shifts

| Concept                | Change Required? | Notes |
|------------------------|------------------|-------|
| Main character logic   | ✅ Remove as structural special case |
| Emotion/mood logic     | ✅ Apply identically to players & NPCs |
| Event targeting        | ✅ Use selector-based targeting |
| UI decision routing    | ✅ Per-player context awareness |
| Story state control    | ✅ Branch resolution per player |
| Debug/testing          | ✅ Track/save/replay per player context |
| Relationship modeling  | ✅ Now includes player-to-player relationships |

---

Would you like:
- A checklist for making existing systems “player-neutral”?
- A story design pattern guide for multi-protagonist narratives?
- Suggestions for how to structure per-player “memory” and private knowledge?

This shift isn’t just structural—it invites **richer narrative possibilities** where every player is a character with story gravity.

assistant
Not explicitly—thank you for calling that out. Let's cover them now, because both **hotseat** and **split screen** are valuable multiplayer modes for narrative-rich, emotionally reactive games like the one you're building.

They deserve specific consideration since they impact UI design, timing models, privacy of character state, and emotional feedback loops.

---

## 🪑 **Hotseat Mode**

### 🧠 Core Concept  
Multiple players take turns on the **same device**, with the game presenting one player's perspective at a time.

### ✅ Strengths  
- Low hardware/network requirements  
- Great for emotional storytelling: players observe each other’s arcs  
- Built-in pacing: natural pauses between turns  
- High compatibility with decision-based games

### ⚠️ Considerations  
| Challenge              | Solution |
|------------------------|----------|
| Privacy of internal states | Temporary screen blanking, turn transition screens, or shared info policies |
| Turn scoping | Use tick-based or player-initiated advancement (e.g., "End Scene" button) |
| UI swapping | Auto-recoloring, profile icons, or player nameplates |

---

## 🖥️ **Split Screen Mode**

### 🧠 Core Concept  
Each player sees their perspective **simultaneously**, either on the same screen or separate windows/devices rendered by the same engine.

### ✅ Strengths  
- Real-time coordination, tension, or parallel storylines  
- Allows overlap of scenes, perception, or location  
- Powerful for exploring **conflicting emotions or misunderstandings**

### ⚠️ Considerations  
| Challenge              | Solution |
|------------------------|----------|
| Screen space | Allow vertical/horizontal splits or detached views |
| Narrative timing | Shared ticks, per-player event queue, or sync windows |
| Performance | Careful scene graph management and UI rendering efficiency |
| Local input mapping | Detect and route controller/keyboard input per screen area or device |

---

## 🔀 Hybrid Option: Hotseat + Split Perspective

You could support **switchable modes** per event or chapter:
- Players hotseat through high-intensity branching scenes
- Split view is enabled during parallel exploration or confrontation scenes

---

## 💡 Use Case Fit (Summary)

| Mode           | Best For |
|----------------|----------|
| **Hotseat**     | Turn-based stories, dialogue-heavy scenes, shared device play |
| **Split Screen**| Real-time branching, emotional divergence, multiplayer drama |
| **Hybrid**      | Episodic pacing, mystery arcs, flexible story control |

---

## 🛠 Engine Support Notes

### In Godot:
- Hotseat is trivial; just UI state switching and player handoff
- Split screen:
  - Use `ViewportContainer` for each screen pane
  - Handle input contexts per player viewport
  - UI skinning or color accents to differentiate player areas
  - Share state or tick via your main `chordline.gd` coordination node

---

Would you like:
- Suggested UI conventions for emotional or mood overlays per player (split or shared)?
- A quick mockup of hotseat turn transitions?
- Guidelines for authors to write scenes that adapt gracefully to hotseat vs split modes?

These modes are perfect for emotionally rich, slow-burn or socially interactive storytelling—and very few engines support them well out of the box. You could make Chordline a leader here.
user
Regarding game data files: some authors are strongly (unreasonably, imo) protective of the runtime game data file contents and would likely push for obfuscation options and a runtime format that can't be trivially reversed to pull thing up in the designer. I'm somewhat inclined to shrug and say "not my problem", but this also seems like something that could be solved with plugins.
assistant
You're spot on—this is one of those **low-priority but high-noise edge concerns**. Most creators *won’t* mind, but a vocal minority absolutely will, and providing a path for them (without entangling your core design with it) is a strategic move.

Your instinct to **delegate this concern to plugins** is the correct one. Here's a breakdown of how to approach it cleanly:

---

## 🔒 The “Protect My Game Data” Concern

### Motivation (reasonable or not):
- Preventing spoilers or plot leaks
- Protecting proprietary dialogue or writing
- Delaying datamining during early access
- “Professional polish” (perceived obfuscation = seriousness)

### Actual Risk:
- Obfuscation only slows down determined users  
- Every layer of protection can be eventually reversed  
- It can hurt modding, accessibility, and trust

---

## ✅ Chordline Core Stance

> **All runtime game data is stored in accessible, well-structured, optionally serialized formats.**  
> Obfuscation is **not included by default** but **fully supported via plugin hooks.**

This keeps the core:
- Simple
- Testable
- Moddable
- Transparent

---

## 🔌 Plugin-Based Obfuscation Strategy

### 🔐 How a plugin can implement it:
1. **Intercept the asset/data export pipeline**  
   - Replace or wrap `.json`, `.yaml`, `.cstore`, etc. with custom format
2. **Use pre-runtime transformation**  
   - Encrypt, compress, or flatten data ahead of time
3. **Patch or override data loading API**  
   - Provide a custom `GameDataLoader` that reverses the transformation

### Example plugin behaviors:
- **Obfuscation only**: lightweight rename + shuffle
- **Compression**: binary pack using zstd or deflate
- **Encryption**: AES with engine-internal key (dev knows this is just a deterrent)
- **Signed integrity**: basic tamper detection without obfuscation
- **Proprietary scene bundle**: load all events/scenes from encrypted blob

---

## 📂 Suggested Hook Points for Plugin Design

| Hook | Description |
|------|-------------|
| `game_data_loader.gd` | Override how data is read at runtime |
| `data_export_pass.gd` | Hook into build/export process to transform files |
| `manifest_filter.gd` | Control visibility of content in author UI (e.g. block story preview) |
| `chordline/tools/viewer_ui.gd` | Obfuscation-aware view toggle or warning |

---

## 🧰 Optional Support Features in Core

To support plugin flexibility without hardcoding anything, the core could:
- Allow **data loader injection** (per save, per authority)
- Expose **content metadata registry** (allowing plugin to override file resolution)
- Use **late binding** for game text and structure (resolved at load)

---

## 🪶 Light Encouragement Toward Transparency

While supporting obfuscation through plugins:
- The **default dev mode** could show a warning if obfuscated data is detected:  
  _"You're using a data protection plugin. This may reduce modding, testing, and accessibility."_
- You can offer a **“debug mirror” plugin** that shows what's loaded, to balance the culture

---

## 📝 Summary

| Design Decision           | Rationale |
|---------------------------|-----------|
| No obfuscation in core    | Keeps system clean, testable, moddable |
| Obfuscation via plugins   | Satisfies edge cases without hurting core |
| Hook points in export/load| Lets authors customize without patching system |
| Transparent default       | Respects openness while allowing flexibility |

---

Would you like:
- A reference plugin scaffold for lightweight obfuscation (e.g. string reversal + index shuffle)?
- Guidelines for writing deterministic loaders (so obfuscated builds can still pass QA tests)?
- A sample metadata-based plugin that hides only certain types of content (e.g. “twist scenes”)?

This keeps you **aligned with open-source values** while still being **pragmatic for commercial adoption**.

## 💬 In-Game Feedback & Bug Reporting (Optional Network Feature)

### 🧠 Core Goals:
- Allow players/testers to **respond to scenes, events, or choices**
- Enable detailed reports without leaving the game
- Funnel structured data to a **developer-controlled endpoint**
- Keep everything **opt-in**, with full control over **scope and submission**

---

## ✳️ Key Use Cases

| Use Case                         | Example |
|----------------------------------|---------|
| 🟢 Scene-level reactions         | "I loved this moment" / "Felt flat" |
| 🐞 Bug reports                   | "This scene triggered twice" or "UI broke here" |
| 🎭 Character-specific feedback   | "I didn't understand their motive here" |
| 📊 Narrative arc perception      | "This branch feels abrupt" |
| 🧪 Playtest session summaries    | Attach report at end of test |

---

## 📦 Feature Layers

### 1. ⭐ **Lightweight Reactions**
- Per-scene/event thumbs up/down, emoji, or 1–5 star feedback
- Optional tagging: “emotional,” “confusing,” “buggy,” “favorite”

### 2. 📝 **Detailed Report Form**
- Freeform text entry
- Auto-attach:
  - Active save slot GUID
  - Character state snapshot
  - Tick timestamp
  - Event ID or scene key
  - Optional screenshot (if supported)

### 3. 🛠️ **Error and Crash Reporting**
- Optional automatic crash dumps or assertion fails
- Send log + recent save snapshot (with player consent)

### 4. 🔐 **Privacy Controls**
- Global + per-save opt-in
- Preview what data will be sent
- Optionally anonymize user or tie to session ID

---

## 🔌 Server Component (Pluggable)

- Simple endpoint: REST or email proxy
- Auth optional: token-based or public post
- Metadata schema with version tags
- Optional dashboard or export to Google Sheets, GitHub Issues, etc.

---

- Let designers flag scenes/events as **"feedback-worthy"**  
  (_e.g., “Request feedback on this branch in playtests”_)
- Review feedback tied to exact event or path (tick + state snapshot)
- Metrics overlay could show number of positive/negative reactions per branch
- Filtering by:
  - Character
  - Player emotion state
  - Tag (e.g., “slow,” “rushed,” “great pacing”)

---

| Mode          | Trigger |
|---------------|---------|
| Passive       | Thumbs-up/thumbs-down buttons shown at end of a scene |
| Interruptive  | Contextual bug report shortcut (e.g. long-press or hotkey) |
| Opt-in Prompt | "Would you like to give feedback on this scene?" |
| Post-session  | Summary prompt: “Any general feedback before ending?” |

## 📊 In-Game Polls: Time-Windowed Extension

### 🧠 Core Concept
Authors define **polls** that are:
- Shown to players **during a specific game window** (tick or date-based)
- Contain 1–n options, with optional tags/metadata
- Submitted anonymously or per-save (opt-in)
- Reviewed **in aggregate**, **per-save**, or **per-branch**

---

## 📦 Authoring Features

### ✅ Poll Definition
Defined in external data or embedded into event metadata:
```yaml
id: trust_test_01
question: "Should Character X be forgiven?"
options:
  - label: "Yes, they deserve another chance"
  - label: "No, that betrayal runs too deep"
  - label: "I’m not sure yet"
start_tick: 300
end_tick: 420
visibility: ["playtest", "demo"]
anonymize: true
linked_event: "act2_confession"
```

### 📅 Triggering Modes
| Type       | Trigger                            |
|------------|------------------------------------|
| **Global** | All saves entering tick 300–420    |
| **Scene-specific** | Triggered on entering a flagged event |
| **Branch-gated** | Only players who took a certain route |
| **Live push** | (If supported) pushed from dev server during session |

---

## 🎮 Player UX

- Seamless popup at appropriate moment  
- Optional skip button  
- May appear with emotion bar or scene summary (for context)  
- Can include developer message (“You’re helping shape future dialogue”)  

### Example:
> **Poll**  
> “How do you feel about Jules after this scene?”  
> 🔘 I trust them more now  
> 🔘 I still don’t know what to think  
> 🔘 I’m ready to see them fall

---

## 🧩 Integration With Other Systems

### Feedback Overlay:
- Polls appear in same style/layer as like/dislike and detailed feedback  
- May be opt-in for playtest saves only

### Design Studio:
- Poll results visible per branch, tick, or scene  
- Filter by locale, version, player emotional profile

### Metrics Dashboard (Server):
- Responses binned by time and version  
- Graphs of shifting sentiment over time (great for live storytelling!)

---

## 🔐 Privacy and Control

| Setting               | Description |
|-----------------------|-------------|
| **Anonymized**        | No player ID or save ID stored |
| **Per-save scoped**   | Linked to player’s emotional context |
| **Author-reviewable only** | Not exposed to community |

---

## 🧰 Server/Data Structure Example

```json
{
  "poll_id": "trust_test_01",
  "response": "I trust them more now",
  "tick": 342,
  "save_guid": "save-983af2",

  "timestamp": "2025-07-02T23:18:00Z"
}
```

Question: with networked features how to allow authors to create restricted sections and content, e.g. to give premium supporters of the author early access to new content, without forcing complete separation of the community.
assistant
Great question—this is where thoughtful integration of **author-controlled access layers** into networked features can offer powerful flexibility *without fracturing the community* or compromising openness. You want to support things like:

- Early access to **new scenes, modules, or narrative arcs**  
- Access-gated **polls, feedback features, or shared saves**  
- Thematic or in-world “vault” areas tied to support status or event participation  

And you want to do it in a way that:
- ✅ Doesn't require maintaining separate game builds  
- ✅ Doesn't alienate or segregate the wider player base  
- ✅ Remains compatible with cloud saves, featured saves, feedback, etc.

---

## 🔐 Access-Controlled Content: System Design

### 🧩 Core Concepts

| Concept             | Purpose |
|---------------------|---------|
| **Access Tag**      | Defines content grouping (e.g., `early_access`, `supporter`, `event_2025`) |
| **Entitlement Map** | Player/session gets a set of access tags via login, import, or config |
| **Conditional Inclusion** | Content can be shown, hidden, or locked behind access tags |
| **Graceful Degradation** | Non-eligible players see narrative alternatives, locked visuals, or optional delay timers |

---

## 🎟️ Access Tag Example

```yaml
id: secret_garden_scene
access_tags: ["supporter_tier_2", "early_access_march"]
fallback_event: "locked_gate_cutscene"
```

A scene like this would only load if the current session has one of the matching tags. Otherwise, the game gracefully loads the fallback.

---

## ✅ Entitlement Options (Author Control)

| Source                   | How it Grants Tags                        | Notes |
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------------|-------|
| 🌐 Authenticated login   | User logs in via author-managed endpoint and receives entitlement map |
| 📁 Entitlement file      | Author distributes access keys, save injectors, or config files |
| 🗂️ Save metadata         | Certain saves (e.g., cloud-saved from a dev preview) carry forward their entitlements |
| 🗓️ Time-based unlock     | Tags can be granted by date (e.g., early access expires after 2 weeks) |
| 🧩 Plugin/script logic    | Mod/plugin manually sets tags (e.g., linked Patreon tier) |

All of these can be **optional** and **engine-agnostic**—driven by plugins, scripts, or a federation of services.

---

## 🧰 How It Shows Up In Game

### UI:
- “Unlocked for Supporters” badge or watermark  
- Visual teaser or placeholder for locked content  
- Optional encouragement: “This scene unlocks for everyone on July 10th”

### In-world analog:
- Gate that requires special token or lore explanation (“You hear music, but the door is closed—for now.”)

---

## 📡 Integration With Network Features

| Feature                | Behavior with Access Tags |
|------------------------|---------------------------|
| **Featured Saves**     | Marked as `requires: ["dlc_summer2025"]`—hidden unless player has access |
| **Polls**              | Some only visible to players with matching access |
| **Feedback**           | Can be scoped so only early-access testers comment on early content |
| **Bug Reports**        | Access tags embedded in reports for developer context |

---

## ⚖️ Community Cohesion Strategy

### ❗Key: Don't fragment, **filter**

- Let all players **see** that gated content exists—but explain *why* they don’t have access.
- Let cloud saves and featured saves **note missing access** (e.g., “This save includes content you don’t have yet, continuing will skip it.”)
- Allow players to **opt into future unlocks** (“Notify me when this arc is public.”)

This keeps the sense of a **shared world** while rewarding access holders with smoother or earlier participation.

---

## ✍️ Author Workflow Summary

1. Define access tags (e.g., `dlc_forest`, `supporter_gold`, `event_november`)
2. Apply them to events, scenes, polls, wiki entries, assets, etc.
3. Set fallback content where needed
4. Choose how to distribute entitlements (login, save files, etc.)
5. Optionally display access notices, watermarks, or expiration hints

---

## ✅ Summary

| Feature                        | Supported? | Notes |
|-------------------------------|------------|-------|
| Per-content access tags        | ✅ Built-in through metadata |
| Flexible entitlement methods   | ✅ Plugin/server/config based |
| Fallbacks for non-access users | ✅ Encouraged for cohesion |
| No forced separation           | ✅ Mixed access is fully supported |
| Mod- and author-friendly       | ✅ System works offline or with hosted servers |

---

Would you like:
- A standard access tag schema and entitlement file format?
- UI mockups for how access-limited content might display?
- Sample plugin architecture for linking to external supporter platforms (e.g., Patreon)?

This system lets authors reward supporters **without creating gated silos**, keeping community shared while respecting creator needs.

Planning for incremental releases: suggestions for making it easy for authors to preserve a complete copy of game state at a certain point and allow some level of play in a sandbox environment, then at the next content drop the sandbox is exited and state retired, possibly with defined partial leakage (e.g. The player can keep up to 100gp earned in sandbox).
assistant
This is a fantastic design challenge, and it touches on both **live narrative development** and **controlled progression systems**. Your goals are clear:

1. Let authors **publish content incrementally** (episodes, chapters, seasonal arcs).
2. Let players **explore existing content freely**—even between updates.
3. Provide a **sandbox mode** where players can interact, test, grind, or play loosely.
4. Cleanly exit sandbox mode on the next content drop, with **limited carryover**.

Done well, this creates:
- A flexible **release cadence** for authors  
- **Engagement opportunities** during lulls between updates  
- A **graceful transition system** that preserves narrative and gameplay integrity

---

## 🔄 System Design: Incremental Releases + Sandbox Carryover

### 🧱 Core Concepts

| Concept              | Purpose |
|----------------------|---------|
| **State Snapshot**   | Frozen save file at update boundary |
| **Sandbox Mode**     | Loosely canonical, low-stakes gameplay session |
| **Leakage Rules**    | Define what transfers into the new mainline content |
| **Retirement Marker**| Ensures sandbox can't continue after next update |

---

## 🗂️ Author-Facing Release Model

### 1. **Snapshot Savepoint (Author-defined)**
At the end of an update cycle, the engine:
- Records a complete copy of the player’s state (`core_save`)
- Tags it with the current game version / content schema
- Offers an automatic branch into sandbox mode (`sandbox_v1`)

```yaml
snapshot:
  id: "v1_release_end"
  label: "End of Chapter 1"
  created_at: "2025-07-05"
  tags: ["official", "leakage_allowed"]
```

---

### 2. **Sandbox Definition**
The author defines:
- Permitted activities (exploration, combat, social events)
- Events excluded from canon (e.g., future-flagged story beats)
- Soft rules for outcomes (e.g., can earn gold, but not trust)
- Optional constraints (e.g., finite loop, degraded relationships, etc.)

```yaml
sandbox_rules:
  allowed_subsystems: ["economy", "appearance", "events:sandbox"]
  forbidden_tags: ["main_plot", "emotion:irreversible"]
  duration_limit_ticks: 300
  decay_multiplier: 2.0
```

---

### 3. **Leakage Definition on Next Content Drop**

Upon new release, authors define **carryover rules** like:
```yaml
leakage_rules:
  max_gold: 100
  keep_inventory: false
  preserve_relationships: ["jules", "cass"]
  merge_appearance: true
  notes: "Sandbox achievements converted to 'Reputation' in Ch2"
```

These rules are applied automatically when importing sandbox saves into the new update. Sandbox saves not imported become marked as **retired** or **archival**.

---

## 🎮 Player UX

### On Content Completion:
- Prompt: “Would you like to enter Sandbox Mode while waiting for the next update?”
- Message: “Progress will be saved, but not all of it will carry over.”
- Save split occurs automatically:
  - `Mainline Save` → canonical, frozen
  - `Sandbox Save` → derived, mutable

### In Sandbox:
- Optional UI indicator ("Sandbox Mode – gold earnings capped at 100gp")
- Players can replay side quests, try alternate outfits, or test builds
- Visual watermark or toggle for developer/test builds if needed

### On Update:
- "Chapter 2 is available! You can continue from your mainline save, or import from your sandbox session (partial rewards apply)."
- Player sees exactly what will transfer
- Old sandbox save becomes locked or flagged as non-canonical

---

## 📊 Technical Architecture

### Suggested Fields to Track:
- `save_type`: `mainline`, `sandbox`, `archived`
- `origin_version`: `v1.2`
- `leakage_flags`: `{gold: 87, relationships: {...}, flags: {...}}`
- `retired_at_version`: `v2.0`

### Carryover Helper Functions:
- `apply_leakage_rules(sandbox_save, leakage_rules)`
- `diff_state(old_mainline, sandbox_state)` → show what changed
- `sandbox_is_active(version)` → block import if not current

---

## 🧰 Optional Tools for Authors

- **Visual state diffing** between mainline and sandbox saves
- **Leakage config previewer**: See what players will retain under current rules
- **Auto-expiry setting**: “Retire all sandbox saves after 30 days or new version”
- **Event tagging aid**: Help classify events as sandbox-eligible or canon-locked

---

## ✅ Summary

| Feature                       | Supported? | Notes |
|-------------------------------|------------|-------|
| Savepoint snapshots           | ✅ Author-defined checkpoints |
| Sandbox mode branching        | ✅ Isolated loop play allowed |
| Leakage rules for carryover   | ✅ Author-controlled, granular |
| Sandbox detection             | ✅ Tag-based + runtime controls |
| Save retirement               | ✅ Soft expiration or lockout |
| Diff viewer (optional)        | 🟡 Author tooling enhancement |
| Player transparency           | ✅ Prompts, UI, preview of carryover |

---

Would you like:
- A sample YAML spec for sandbox save and leakage rule formats?
- UI wireframes for players transitioning between states?
- A playtesting use case where sandbox becomes a “closed beta arc”?

This feature **keeps players engaged during downtime** and **gives authors full control over continuity**—without needing to fragment saves or builds. Perfect for episodic, early-access, or live storytelling workflows.
user
On reflection, some authors may want to retain the sandbox after content updates as a skipple thing.
assistant
Excellent insight—and very wise to accommodate this upfront. Letting **sandbox saves persist after content updates** and offering them as a **skippable alternative to canonical play** opens up several high-value uses:

- Replayability without narrative reset  
- Skip-ahead paths for returning players  
- Preview/future-timeline experimentation (e.g. “What if I kept this version of the world?”)  
- Dev/test modes or **“nonlinear continuity” games** (like *80 Days*, *Heaven’s Vault*, *Disco Elysium*)  

Instead of treating sandbox saves as disposable, we can **elevate them to “alternate continuity branches”**—with author-defined controls.

---

## ✅ Expanded Model: Persistent Sandbox as Skippable Timeline

### 📦 Save Metadata Fields (Updated)

```yaml
type: sandbox
origin_snapshot: "ch1_release_end"
retired: false
is_branchable: true
leakage_applied: false
canonical_override_allowed: true
notes: "Author allows players to continue this sandbox past the update"
```

---

## 🧠 Player Options on Content Drop

When new content is released, players with a matching sandbox save see:

> “You’ve been playing in a sandbox from Chapter 1. You may:
>
> 1. **Return to Canon**  
>    Resume from your last canonical save and continue the main story.
>
> 2. **Import Sandbox Progress** *(with limits)*  
>    Bring your sandbox state into the canon timeline using author-defined carryover rules.
>
> 3. **Continue as a Parallel Timeline**  
>    Keep playing from your sandbox as its own alternate world. New content will unlock, but results may differ from canon.”

---

## 🗺️ Timeline Representation (Optional)

Let players **see their branching history**:

```
Chapter 1 (Mainline)
  ├── Canon Save → Chapter 2 Start
  └── Sandbox A (active)
         ├── Imported → Chapter 2 (with carryover)
         └── Timeline B (parallel sandbox continues)
```

The **engine supports loading both**, but flags one as “official.”

---

## 🧩 Author Controls: Enabling Persistent Sandboxes

Authors can now choose per-update:

```yaml
allow_sandbox_branching: true
sandbox_behavior:
  keep_open_after_update: true
  allow_full_content: true
  warn_if_noncanonical: true
  leakage_option_prompt: true
  sandbox_flag_in_ui: true
  impact_on_metrics: "separate"
```

---

## 🛠 Engine Behavior and Tooling

| System               | Adjustments |
|----------------------|-------------|
| **Save loader**      | Distinguish `mainline`, `sandbox (retired)`, `sandbox (active)`, and `branch` saves |
| **Narrative engine** | Accept sandbox state unless explicitly blocked |
| **Designer tooling** | Let events flag themselves as “skip-safe” or “canon-only” |
| **Feedback/polls**   | Can report separately for canon vs sandbox branches |
| **Save UI**          | Show sandbox branches, allow naming and filtering |

---

## 🧰 Benefits of Keeping Sandboxes Open

| For Authors                          | For Players                        |
|--------------------------------------|------------------------------------|
| Less pressure to perfect carryover rules | Replay without restart             |
| Test arcs or future episodes early   | Preserve experimental character paths |
| Reuse sandboxes for future features  | Continue even if canon story stalls |
| Allow meta or optional content       | Roleplay alternate moral routes     |

---

## 🔐 Optional Restrictions

If an author *wants* to limit the sandbox’s reach:

- Cap future chapter access (e.g., “sandbox stops after Chapter 2”)  
- Flag branches as **non-exportable**, **non-shareable**, or **non-metric**  
- Require replay for full ending unlocks  
- Use **in-world narrative framing**: “This is the world that might have been...”

---

## ✅ Summary

| Feature                             | Support? | Notes |
|-------------------------------------|----------|-------|
| Sandbox preservation post-update    | ✅ Built into updated metadata |
| Player branch selection             | ✅ With full control and prompts |
| Author toggles for allowed behavior | ✅ Skip-safe vs canon-locked |
| Metrics separation                  | ✅ Optional per flag or mode |
| Save timeline UI (optional)         | 🟡 Highly recommended for clarity |
| Role in narrative replay/flexibility| ✅ Excellent for emotional or episodic stories |

---

Would you like:
- UI mockups for timeline selection or sandbox prompts?
- Suggested design rules for skip-safe content and alternate branches?
- Examples from games that use alternate timelines or sandbox-as-canon tricks?

This approach ensures Chordline can support everything from **tight linear games** to **fluid, emergent, or branching alternate worlds**—without locking the engine or player into a single style.
user
In incremental data file updates: how could the system itself help create minimal update deltas for multiple prior versions? Maybe if game data files are plain text could just be standard unified diff format, ideally from git tags or some such.
assistant
That’s a sharp idea—and absolutely feasible. Supporting **minimal update deltas** from multiple prior versions is highly desirable for:

- 🐢 **Low-bandwidth or constrained environments**  
- 🛠 **Modular patching workflows**  
- 🧪 **Deterministic reproduction and rollback for debugging**  
- ♻️ **Efficient asset reuse across branches, forks, and updates**  

If your **game data files are plain text** (e.g., YAML, JSON, or similar structured markup), then:

> 📦 **Using standard diff tools (like unified diff format) is not only viable—it’s smart.**

Let’s break it down:

---

## ✅ Strategy: Delta Updates via Versioned Text Data + Unified Diffs

### 🧩 Core Assumptions:
- Game data files are plaintext (YAML/JSON/DSL)
- Engine supports **deterministic loading** from file content, not internal version state
- Each game version is tagged (e.g. via Git or external manifest)
- Authors are willing to publish from VCS-like sources or snapshots

---

## 🗃️ File Versioning & Tagging (Baseline)

Encourage authors to:
- Maintain content in a **versioned text repo** (Git recommended)
- Tag releases: `v1.0`, `v1.1`, `v2.0-beta`, etc.
- Keep each release's data in a stable, structured hierarchy:  
  `scenes/`, `characters/`, `events/`, `metadata/`, etc.

Then, **use standard tools** to compute diffs between tagged versions.

---

## 🔀 Diff Format Recommendation

| Format         | Reason to Use                       |
|----------------|--------------------------------------|
| **Unified diff (`.patch`)** | Readable, supported by `git apply`, small |
| JSON Patch (RFC 6902) | Precise structural changes, ideal for APIs |
| Custom delta encoding (optional) | More compact, but more work to support |
| Binary diff (e.g., `bsdiff`) | Only if you allow binary blobs—avoid for game logic files |

For your use case:  
> ✅ **Unified diff format** is ideal for YAML/JSON sources, human review, and automated application via common tools.

---

## 🔁 Runtime or Preload Integration Options

| Model | Description |
|-------|-------------|
| **Offline pre-merge** | Installer or launcher applies diff before launch, outputs new data |
| **Runtime merge** | Engine applies diffs on top of cached base data (slower, useful for sandbox) |
| **Snapshot rebase** | When loading a save from `v1.2`, engine applies `v1.2 ➝ v2.0` deltas before continuing |
| **Streaming update** | Pull only diffs from update server, apply just-in-time for sandbox or episodic play |

---

## 🧰 Tools You Can Provide

### 1. **Delta Export Utility**
- Run `chordline export-delta --from-tag v1.2 --to-tag v2.0`
- Produces a `delta_v1.2_to_v2.0.patch` or zipped patch bundle
- Optional: also generate a manifest of affected scenes/events/assets

### 2. **Delta Application System**
- `apply_patch(original_folder, patch_file) → new_folder`
- Can be a command-line tool or integrated in the launcher/game engine
- Optional “dry-run”/verify mode: shows which files would change

### 3. **Save Compatibility Mapper**
- Given a save from v1.2, suggests or auto-applies the required delta chain to bring it to current
- May fetch delta bundles remotely or use local cache

---

## 🧠 Advanced Add-ons

| Feature                      | Purpose |
|-----------------------------|---------|
| **Patch chaining support**  | Allow `v1.0➝v1.1`, `v1.1➝v1.2`, … to stack |  
| **Fallbacks / rollbacks**   | Let players revert (with warnings) |
| **Conflict detection**      | Warn if save contains scene/event IDs that no longer exist |
| **Per-subsystem delta support** | Diff only `emotion/`, `events/`, etc. for modular patches |

---

## 📝 Summary Table

| Component                    | Suggested Approach |
|-----------------------------|--------------------|
| File format                 | Plaintext (YAML/JSON) |
| Diff format                 | Unified diff (`.patch`) or JSON Patch |
| Diff creation               | Git tags or tool-generated snapshots |
| Application method          | Pre-launch merge or on-demand |
| Optional automation         | Patch manager, save upgrader, changelog visualizer |
| Risk level                  | Low, especially if schema versioning is stable |
| Compatibility               | Excellent for open-source, versioned pipelines, collaborative mods |

---

## 🧩 Author UX Flow Example

```shell
$ chordline tag-release v1.2
$ chordline export-delta --from v1.2 --to v2.0 --out deltas/v1.2_to_v2.0.patch
$ chordline apply-delta --base game_data_v1.2/ --patch deltas/v1.2_to_v2.0.patch --out game_data_v2.0/
```

This plays beautifully with Git-based versioning, CI/CD, mod support, and archival.

---

Would you like:
- A reference delta export format in unified diff style?
- A CLI utility spec for generating/applying deltas?
- Example YAML of changelog metadata embedded in deltas?

This approach gives you **lean, version-flexible updates** without locking authors into proprietary pipelines—exactly the kind of future-friendly design Chordline is being built around.
user
How might the system softly encourage the authors to use version control for the game data?
assistant
Encouraging authors to adopt version control—**without mandating it**—is a subtle balance of good UX, gentle nudges, and practical incentives. Here’s a strategy to make version control feel like the *natural*, low-friction way to work in Chordline:

---

## 🧠 Core Strategy: "Softly Opinionated"

Design the system to **work best** with version control, provide **benefits** that are unlocked when it's used, and avoid breaking functionality when it’s not.

---

## ✅ Tactics to Softly Encourage VCS Adoption

### 1. **Auto-detection of Git Repos**
- On project open, detect if `.git/` is present.
- If not, show a **non-blocking notification**:
  > “This project is not under version control. Set up Git to enable timeline diffs, rollback support, and easier updates.”

- Button: `📘 Learn why` or `🔧 Initialize Git`

---

### 2. **Built-in Git Integration (Optional Plugin)**
- Offer first-class UI for:
  - Commit/push
  - Branch switching
  - Viewing diffs between tags or commits
- Label VCS-powered features clearly:  
  _“Export delta (Git tag: v1.2 → v2.0)”_

This frames version control as **a creative tool**, not a technical burden.

---

### 3. **Enhanced Tools When Git is Enabled**
Provide minor but *real* feature upgrades when Git is present:
| Feature | With Git | Without Git |
|--------|----------|-------------|
| **Delta export** | Tag-to-tag diffs, named updates | Full folder scan only |
| **Timeline view** | Annotated commit history | Manual savepoints only |
| **Rollback** | True version rollback | Save-based rollback only |
| **Save snapshot stamping** | Auto-versioning with commit hash | Manual labeling required |
| **Changelog previews** | From Git diff | Only available if manually written |

This encourages adoption by **showing value**, not withholding basics.

---

### 4. **Author Dashboard "Best Practices" Checklist**
Include a non-enforced checklist on the project home screen:
- [x] Version control initialized  
- [ ] Latest commit tagged  
- [ ] Changelog for last release created  
- [x] Savepoints snapshot before update

Let the system quietly **check these automatically** and reward completion with:
- "Project in good shape" badge  
- Easier delta packaging  
- Clean UX for release packaging

---

### 5. **Project Templates Come Pre-Initialized**
All new project templates ship with:
- `.gitignore` configured for game builds, saves, and logs  
- Option to initialize a Git repo at creation  
- A dummy changelog and README.md  
- Optional Git hook to sync version tag with changelog entries

This frames versioning as **just part of making a Chordline game**, not a developer-only feature.

---

### 6. **“Restore Point” and “Delta” Tools Gently Lean on VCS**
- Savepoint UI prefers named Git tags (if present)
- Diff tool says:  
  > _"We noticed this project isn't under version control. Without Git, we can still show a diff, but can't generate update deltas automatically."_  
  > _"Enable Git to unlock automatic version diffing."_  
- When authors try to package a patch, suggest tagging the prior state first

---

## 📝 Bonus: Narrative Framing

Present Git not as a coder tool, but a **story engine timeline**:

> _“Use versioning to track alternate timelines of your narrative, test multiple endings, or fork a playtest path without losing your core story.”_

This makes it appealing to **authors** and **narrative designers**, not just developers.

---

## ✅ Summary Table

| Soft Encouragement Technique    | Purpose |
|----------------------------------|---------|
| Detect + notify if Git absent   | Awareness |
| Provide useful extras if Git present | Motivation |
| Tag-based delta/export tooling  | Functionality unlock |
| Pre-initialized project templates | Lower barrier |
| Best-practice checklist         | Visible goals |
| Git-as-story-framing            | Emotional buy-in |

---

Would you like:
- A template `.gitignore` and default project Git config?
- UI mockups of the Git-aware delta/export tools?
- Language suggestions for the soft prompts and nudges?

This approach makes version control a **first-class design assistant**, not a chore. And when authors start seeing Git as a *storytelling timeline* tool, many will wonder how they ever shipped without it.

Stretch feature: integrated game design bible?
assistant
Absolutely—an **integrated game design bible** is a powerful stretch feature that aligns beautifully with Chordline’s ambitions. Rather than being an external document (as is typical), this system embeds the **author’s design intent, lore, mechanics, and references** into the engine itself—accessible **at design time**, **runtime**, and **debug time**.

---

## 📖 What Is an Integrated Game Design Bible?

A **living, searchable, versioned reference system** embedded in the game project that:

- Centralizes narrative, system, and worldbuilding documentation  
- Cross-links directly to game content (events, characters, mechanics)  
- Evolves with the project, supports collaboration and QA  
- Can optionally be surfaced in-game or to modders

---

## ✨ Key Features

| Feature                         | Description |
|----------------------------------|-------------|
| 🧠 **Author-facing docs**         | Structured design entries for narrative, emotion systems, branching logic, pacing notes, etc. |
| 🧩 **Entity cross-linking**       | Link to scenes, characters, emotional states, save logic, etc. (e.g., `"See also: scene/duel_at_garden"`) |
| 🏷️ **Tags & categories**          | Sort entries by theme: `characters`, `timeline`, `emotion-model`, `rules`, `visual-style` |
| 🕓 **Version & history tracking** | Track how a concept or rule evolved (optionally powered by Git) |
| 🔍 **Searchable in-studio UI**    | Fast lookup from within the design tools |
| 🎮 **Runtime access (opt-in)**    | In-game codex, unlockable lore entries, developer-only debug overlays |
| 💾 **Exportable**                 | Generate PDFs, markdown, or interactive web docs from bible content |
| 🔒 **Access roles**               | Distinguish between dev notes, public-facing lore, QA annotations, etc. |

---

## 🧱 Suggested Structure

```yaml
design_bible/
  characters/
    cassandra.yaml
    jules.yaml
  lore/
    veilguard_origins.md
    magic_system.yaml
  systems/
    emotion_model.md
    save_architecture.md
  writing_guides/
    tone.md
    localization_tips.md
  changelog/
    patch_notes_v1.3.yaml
```

Each entry could include:

```yaml
id: jules
type: character
aliases: ["Julian", "The Stag"]
summary: |
  Jules is a former noble turned insurgent. Cold exterior, emotionally volatile underneath.
related_scenes: ["duel_at_garden", "confession_evening"]
emotional_profile:
  baseline_trust: -10
  max_jealousy: 70
voice_notes: "Reserved. Weighted delivery. Softens around Cass."
localization_flags: ["formal", "gender-neutral"]
```

---

- Hover a character name → preview their profile from the bible  
- Click a scene ID → open linked bible entries for lore or emotional targets  
- Emotion model editor shows design notes on trust, jealousy, etc.  
- Poll results or feedback can include `"related_bible_refs"` for developer review

- Spellcheck, term glossary enforcement  
- AI assistant for auto-suggesting cross-links or summarizing branches (optional)

---

|-------------|-----|

user
What is the synergy level between the wiki and game design bible? Can they be provided by the same plugin? Should they be?
assistant
The **synergy between the wiki and the game design bible** is *very high*—they both deal with structured, cross-referenced, evolving information that informs development, supports runtime access, and reflects the internal logic of the game world. But they serve **slightly different roles**, and whether they should be combined into a single plugin depends on your priorities around clarity, modularity, and user expectations.

---

## 🧠 Conceptual Comparison

| Aspect              | Wiki                                      | Design Bible                                 |
|---------------------|-------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|
| **Primary audience**| Players (in-game) + optionally devs       | Developers, writers, localizers, QA          |
| **Purpose**         | In-world knowledge and references         | Out-of-world documentation and intent        |
| **Tone**            | Diegetic, world-consistent                | Editorial, technical, explanatory            |
| **Scope**           | Characters, places, items, events (player-facing) | Systems, emotions, arcs, narrative rules (dev-facing) |
| **Access timing**   | Primarily runtime, optionally dev-time    | Primarily dev-time, optionally runtime       |
| **Modifiability**   | May be partially revealed/unlocked in play | Editable only via studio tools or script     |

---

## 🔗 Synergy Highlights

- **Shared infrastructure**:
  - Search index
  - Tagging and categories
  - Localization
  - Cross-links between entries
  - Asset attachment (images, sounds, etc.)
  - Version tracking

- **Useful interactions**:
  - Bible can cite or include wiki entries as “in-universe” evidence
  - Wiki entries can have metadata sourced from the bible (e.g. canonical name, role, affiliations)
  - Both may annotate scenes, characters, or emotional events

---

## 🧩 Should They Use the Same Plugin?

### ✅ Yes — If:

- You want to reduce implementation complexity  
- You want a **unified UX** for documentation  
- You expect **some overlap in data (e.g., shared entry formats)**  
- You're targeting smaller teams who benefit from **consolidation**

You can provide **two distinct views**:
- `Wiki View`: filtered to in-universe entries, structured for runtime
- `Design Bible View`: full access, additional system tabs, editorial notes, and narrative scaffolding

---

### ❌ No — If:

- You want **strict architectural separation** between runtime and dev-only data  
- You anticipate **external or modular authoring pipelines** (e.g., Google Docs → Bible, in-game unlocks → Wiki)  
- You want to let **modders edit the wiki but not touch the bible**  
- You expect the Bible to grow complex with versioning, dev-only fields, design schemas, etc.

In this case, separate plugins that **share common services** (e.g., indexing, asset storage, entry linking) is cleaner.

---

## ⚙️ Suggested Hybrid Architecture

Even if separated logically, they can share:

| Component            | Shared? |
|----------------------|---------|
| Data format (YAML/JSON) | ✅ (with type-specific fields) |
| Index/search service     | ✅ Unified plugin service |
| Asset storage (images, voice refs) | ✅ Common folder |
| Localization hooks       | ✅ Built-in i18n tags and structure |
| UI framework (list/detail panels) | ✅ Reused components |
| Runtime entry loader     | 🟡 Optional—only for wiki |
| Tag/category structure   | ✅ May overlap partially |

---

## 🧩 Naming / Plugin Model Options

| Model                | Description |
|----------------------|-------------|
| **Single plugin: `knowledge_base`** | Provides both wiki and bible views; entries marked with `visibility: [player, author]` etc. |
| **Core + extensions** | `chordline_wiki_core`, with optional `wiki_ui_runtime`, `design_bible_author_ui` plugins |
| **Separate plugins, shared backend** | Clean plugin separation, but shared base service like `chordline_knowledge_index` |

---

## 📝 Summary

| Question                         | Answer |
|----------------------------------|--------|
| **Is there high synergy?**       | ✅ Yes—shared structure, tools, and cross-linking |
| **Can they share a plugin?**     | ✅ Yes, with strong UX clarity |
| **Should they share a plugin?**  | 🤔 Depends on scale, audience, and data access expectations |
| **What’s the middle ground?**    | Shared backend + distinct views or access modes |

---

Would you like:
- A sample schema for an entry that includes both wiki and design fields?
- A suggested UI layout for a combined “entry viewer” with runtime/author tabs?
- Plugin/module breakdown suggestions for staged implementation?

Combining the two thoughtfully can give you a **truly author-first worldbuilding and system design platform**, while keeping the door open for in-game codexes, lore exploration, and structured dev documentation.
